						<div id="contentSub"><div id="mw-content-subtitle"></div></div>
						
					</div>
					
					<div id="mw-content-text" class="mw-body-content"><div class="mw-content-ltr mw-parser-output" lang="en" dir="ltr" data-mw-parsoid-version="0.24.0.0-alpha27" data-mw-html-version="2.8.0"><section data-mw-section-id="0" id="mwAQ"><div class="shortdescription nomobile noexcerpt noprint searchaux" style="display:none" about="#mwt1" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mwAg" data-mw='{"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"short description","href":"./Template:Short_description"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"Small tablet with a curse written on it from the Greco-Roman world"}},"i":0}}]}'>Small tablet with a curse written on it from the Greco-Roman world</div><span class="mw-empty-elt" about="#mwt1"><link rel="mw:PageProp/Category" href="./Category:Articles_with_short_description"/><link rel="mw:PageProp/Category" href="./Category:Short_description_is_different_from_Wikidata"/></span>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb" id="mwAw"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Tablette_de_défixion_d'Eyguières.jpg" class="mw-file-description" id="mwBA"><img resource="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Tablette_de_défixion_d'Eyguières.jpg" src="//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/02/Tablette_de_d%C3%A9fixion_d%27Eygui%C3%A8res.jpg/250px-Tablette_de_d%C3%A9fixion_d%27Eygui%C3%A8res.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail" decoding="async" loading="lazy" srcset="//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/02/Tablette_de_d%C3%A9fixion_d%27Eygui%C3%A8res.jpg/500px-Tablette_de_d%C3%A9fixion_d%27Eygui%C3%A8res.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail 2x" data-file-width="1905" data-file-height="1533" data-file-type="bitmap" height="201" width="250" class="mw-file-element" id="mwBQ"/></a><figcaption id="mwBg">Eyguieres curse tablet</figcaption></figure>
<p id="mwBw">A <b id="mwCA">curse tablet</b> (<a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_language" title="Latin language" about="#mwt2" typeof="mw:Transclusion" class="mw-redirect" id="mwCQ" data-mw='{"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"langx","href":"./Template:Langx"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"la"},"2":{"wt":"tabella defixionis, defixio"}},"i":0}}]}'>Latin</a><span about="#mwt2">: </span><i lang="la" about="#mwt2">tabella defixionis, defixio</i><link rel="mw:PageProp/Category" href="./Category:Articles_containing_Latin-language_text" about="#mwt2" id="mwCg"/>; <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language" title="Greek language" about="#mwt3" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mwCw" data-mw='{"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"langx","href":"./Template:Langx"},"params":{"1":{"wt":"el"},"2":{"wt":"κατάδεσμος"},"3":{"wt":"katadesmos"}},"i":0}}]}'>Greek</a><span about="#mwt3">: </span><span lang="el" about="#mwt3">κατάδεσμος</span><span about="#mwt3">, </span><small about="#mwt3"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Greek" title="Romanization of Greek">romanized</a>:<span typeof="mw:Entity"> </span></small><span title="Greek-language romanization" about="#mwt3"><i lang="el-Latn">katadesmos</i></span><link rel="mw:PageProp/Category" href="./Category:Articles_containing_Greek-language_text" about="#mwt3" id="mwDA"/>) is a small tablet with a <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curse" title="Curse" id="mwDQ">curse</a> written on it from the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Roman_world" title="Greco-Roman world" id="mwDg">Greco-Roman world</a>. Its name originated from the Greek and Latin words for "pierce"<sup about="#mwt6" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-1" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-1"}}'><a href="#cite_note-1" id="mwDw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwEA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwEQ">[</span>1<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwEg">]</span></span></a></sup> and "bind". The tablets were used to ask the gods, place spirits, or the deceased to perform an action on a person or object, or otherwise compel the subject of the curse.</p>

<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" id="mwEw" data-mw='{"autoGenerated":true}'/></section><section data-mw-section-id="1" id="mwFA" aria-labelledby="Description"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Description">Description</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Curse_tablet&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Description"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb" id="mwFQ"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Roman_baths_2014_57.jpg" class="mw-file-description" id="mwFg"><img resource="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Roman_baths_2014_57.jpg" src="//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/24/Roman_baths_2014_57.jpg/250px-Roman_baths_2014_57.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail" decoding="async" loading="lazy" srcset="//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/24/Roman_baths_2014_57.jpg/500px-Roman_baths_2014_57.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail 2x" data-file-width="5184" data-file-height="3456" data-file-type="bitmap" height="167" width="250" class="mw-file-element mw-file-upright" style="--mw-file-upright: 1" id="mwFw"/></a><figcaption id="mwGA">One of the 130 <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bath_curse_tablets" title="Bath curse tablets" id="mwGQ">Bath curse tablets</a>. The inscription in <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Latin" title="British Latin" id="mwGg">British Latin</a> translates as: "May he who carried off Vilbia from me become liquid as the water. May she who so obscenely devoured her become dumb"<sup about="#mwt9" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-ED_2-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"ED"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-ED-2"}}'><a href="#cite_note-ED-2" id="mwGw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwHA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwHQ">[</span>2<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwHg">]</span></span></a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p id="mwHw">Curse tablets are typically very thin sheets of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lead" title="Lead" id="mwIA">lead</a> with the text scratched on in tiny letters. They were then often rolled, folded, or pierced with nails, and the tablets were then usually placed beneath the ground: either buried in graves or tombs, thrown into wells or pools, sequestered in underground sanctuaries, or nailed to the walls of temples.  Tablets were also used for love spells and when used in this manner they were placed inside the home of the desired target.<sup about="#mwt10" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGager199218_3-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEGager199218"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEGager199218-3"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"Sfn ","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":" Gager "},"2":{"wt":" 1992 "},"p":{"wt":"18"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGager199218-3" id="mwIQ"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwIg"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwIw">[</span>3<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwJA">]</span></span></a></sup>  They are sometimes discovered along with small dolls or figurines (sometimes inaccurately referred to as "<a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitian_Vodou#Reception" title="Haitian Vodou" id="mwJQ">Voodoo dolls</a>"<sup about="#mwt13" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFaraone19914_4-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEFaraone19914"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEFaraone19914-4"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"Sfn ","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":" Faraone "},"2":{"wt":" 1991 "},"p":{"wt":"4"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFaraone19914-4" id="mwJg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwJw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwKA">[</span>4<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwKQ">]</span></span></a></sup>), which may also be pierced by nails. The figurines resembled the target and often had both their feet and hands bound.<sup about="#mwt16" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGager199215_5-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEGager199215"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEGager199215-5"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"Sfn ","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":" Gager "},"2":{"wt":" 1992 "},"p":{"wt":"15"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGager199215-5" id="mwKg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwKw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwLA">[</span>5<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwLQ">]</span></span></a></sup>  Curse tablets also included hair or pieces of clothing. This was seen as a way to give them strength through inclusion of the central essence (Greek: <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ousia" title="Ousia" id="mwLg">Ousia</a>) of the person. This is especially the case in love spells, which calls for "hair from the head of the love target." Some love spells have even been discovered "folded around some hair," probably to bind the spell itself.<sup about="#mwt19" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGager199216–7_6-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEGager199216–7"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEGager199216–7-6"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"Sfn ","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":" Gager "},"2":{"wt":" 1992 "},"pp":{"wt":"16–7"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGager199216–7-6" id="mwLw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwMA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwMQ">[</span>6<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwMg">]</span></span></a></sup>  "Not all tablets included a personal name, but it is clear especially in the Roman period, that tablets were sometimes prepared in advance, with space left for inserting the names provided by paying customers."<sup about="#mwt22" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGager199214_7-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEGager199214"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEGager199214-7"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"Sfn ","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":" Gager "},"2":{"wt":" 1992 "},"p":{"wt":"14"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGager199214-7" id="mwMw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwNA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwNQ">[</span>7<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwNg">]</span></span></a></sup> The cursing rituals may also have incorporated physical binding gestures and spoken elements.<sup about="#mwt29" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-8" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-8"}}'><a href="#cite_note-8" id="mwNw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwOA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwOQ">[</span>8<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwOg">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

<p id="mwOw">The texts on curse tablets are typically addressed to infernal or <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liminal_deity" title="Liminal deity" id="mwPA">liminal</a> gods such as <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pluto_(mythology)" title="Pluto (mythology)" class="mw-redirect" id="mwPQ">Pluto</a>, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charon" title="Charon" id="mwPg">Charon</a>, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hecate" title="Hecate" id="mwPw">Hecate</a>, and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persephone" title="Persephone" id="mwQA">Persephone</a>, sometimes via the mediation of a dead person (probably the corpse in whose grave the tablet was deposited). Some texts do not invoke the gods, however, but merely list the targets of the curse, the crimes or conditions upon which the curse is valid, and/or the intended ill to befall them. Some tablets are inscribed with nothing more than the names of the targets, leading to the supposition that an oral spell may have accompanied the manufacture of the curse.<sup about="#mwt30" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOgden1999xxx_9-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEOgden1999xxx"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEOgden1999xxx-9"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"Sfn ","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":" Ogden "},"2":{"wt":" 1999 "},"p":{"wt":"xxx"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOgden1999xxx-9" id="mwQQ"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwQg"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwQw">[</span>9<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwRA">]</span></span></a></sup> The texts on the tablets were not always curses; tablets were also used to help the dead.  Those at whose grave sites these were placed had usually died at a very young age or in a violent manner, and the tablet was supposed to help lay their souls to rest in spite of their untimely deaths.<sup about="#mwt33" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGager199219_10-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEGager199219"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEGager199219-10"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"Sfn ","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":" Gager "},"2":{"wt":" 1992 "},"p":{"wt":"19"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGager199219-10" id="mwRQ"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwRg"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwRw">[</span>10<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwSA">]</span></span></a></sup>  The language of those texts that do give context is often concerned with justice, either listing the target's crimes in great detail, handing over responsibility for their punishment to the gods, using indefinite grammar. Frequently, such curse tablets are also inscribed with additional, otherwise meaningless "curse" words such as <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barbarous_name" title="Barbarous name" id="mwSQ">Bazagra</a>, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barbarous_name" title="Barbarous name" id="mwSg">Bescu</a>, or <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barbarous_name" title="Barbarous name" id="mwSw">Berebescu</a>, seemingly in order to lend them a kind of supernatural efficacy.</p>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb" id="mwTA"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Curse_tablet_BM_1934.11-5.1.jpg" class="mw-file-description" id="mwTQ"><img resource="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Curse_tablet_BM_1934.11-5.1.jpg" src="//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a0/Curse_tablet_BM_1934.11-5.1.jpg/250px-Curse_tablet_BM_1934.11-5.1.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail" decoding="async" loading="lazy" srcset="//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a0/Curse_tablet_BM_1934.11-5.1.jpg/500px-Curse_tablet_BM_1934.11-5.1.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail 2x" data-file-width="2316" data-file-height="1737" data-file-type="bitmap" height="188" width="250" class="mw-file-element" id="mwTg"/></a><figcaption id="mwTw">Curse tablet found in London. Inscription reads: "I curse Tretia Maria and her life and mind and memory and liver and lungs mixed up together, and her words, thoughts and memory; thus may she be unable to speak what things are concealed, nor be able." (translation: British Museum)</figcaption></figure>
<p id="mwUA">Many of those discovered at Athens refer to <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athenian_law_court_(classical_period)" title="Athenian law court (classical period)" class="mw-redirect" id="mwUQ">court</a> cases and curse the opposing litigant, asking ("May he...") that he botch his performance in court, forget his words, become dizzy and so forth. Others include erotic binding-spells, and spells ranged against thieves, and business and sporting rivals. Those curse tablets targeted at thieves or other criminals may have been more public, and more acceptable; some scholars even refuse to apply the word "curse" to such "positive" texts, preferring expressions such as "judicial prayers".<sup about="#mwt36" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVersnel1991_11-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEVersnel1991"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEVersnel1991-11"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"Sfn ","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":" Versnel "},"2":{"wt":" 1991"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVersnel1991-11" id="mwUg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwUw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwVA">[</span>11<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwVQ">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

<p id="mwVg">In 1979/1980, the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bath_curse_tablets" title="Bath curse tablets" id="mwVw">Bath curse tablets</a> were found at the site of <i id="mwWA"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aquae_Sulis" title="Aquae Sulis" id="mwWQ">Aquae Sulis</a></i> (now <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bath,_Somerset" title="Bath, Somerset" id="mwWg">Bath</a> in England).<sup about="#mwt41" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-12" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-12"}}'><a href="#cite_note-12" id="mwWw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwXA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwXQ">[</span>12<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwXg">]</span></span></a></sup> All but one of the 130 tablets concerned the restitution of stolen goods.<sup about="#mwt44" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-13" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-13"}}'><a href="#cite_note-13" id="mwXw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwYA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwYQ">[</span>13<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwYg">]</span></span></a></sup> Over 80 similar tablets have been discovered in and about the remains of a temple to <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercury_(mythology)" title="Mercury (mythology)" id="mwYw">Mercury</a> nearby, at West Hill, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uley" title="Uley" id="mwZA">Uley</a>,<sup about="#mwt47" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-14" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-14"}}'><a href="#cite_note-14" id="mwZQ"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwZg"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwZw">[</span>14<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwaA">]</span></span></a></sup> making south-western Britain one of the major centres for finds of Latin <i id="mwaQ">defixiones</i>.</p>

<p id="mwag">In <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Egypt" title="Ancient Egypt" id="mwaw">Ancient Egypt</a>, so-called "<a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Execration_texts" title="Execration texts" id="mwbA">Execration Texts</a>" appear around the time of the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelfth_Dynasty_of_Egypt" title="Twelfth Dynasty of Egypt" id="mwbQ">12th Dynasty</a>, listing the names of enemies written on clay figurines or pottery which were then smashed and buried beneath a building under construction (so that they were symbolically "smothered"), or in a cemetery.<sup about="#mwt50" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-15" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-15"}}'><a href="#cite_note-15" id="mwbg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwbw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwcA">[</span>15<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwcQ">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

</section><section data-mw-section-id="2" id="mwcg" aria-labelledby="Voces_mysticae"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Voces_mysticae"><i id="mwcw">Voces mysticae</i></h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Curse_tablet&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Voces mysticae"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb" id="mwdA"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Vervloekingstablet_in_lood_gericht_tegen_Caius_Iulius_Viator.jpg" class="mw-file-description" id="mwdQ"><img resource="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Vervloekingstablet_in_lood_gericht_tegen_Caius_Iulius_Viator.jpg" src="//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/58/Vervloekingstablet_in_lood_gericht_tegen_Caius_Iulius_Viator.jpg/250px-Vervloekingstablet_in_lood_gericht_tegen_Caius_Iulius_Viator.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail" decoding="async" loading="lazy" srcset="//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/58/Vervloekingstablet_in_lood_gericht_tegen_Caius_Iulius_Viator.jpg/500px-Vervloekingstablet_in_lood_gericht_tegen_Caius_Iulius_Viator.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail 2x" data-file-width="4928" data-file-height="3280" data-file-type="bitmap" height="166" width="250" class="mw-file-element" id="mwdg"/></a><figcaption id="mwdw">Roman curse tablet with <i id="mweA">voces mysticae</i> in Greek. The name of the target, Caius Iulius Viator, was added in Latin. Found in Tongeren (Belgium), 70-100 CE, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallo-Roman_Museum,_Tongeren" title="Gallo-Roman Museum, Tongeren" id="mweQ">Gallo-Roman Museum (Tongeren)</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p id="mweg"><i id="mwew"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voces_mysticae" title="Voces mysticae" class="mw-redirect" id="mwfA">Voces mysticae</a></i> are words not immediately recognizable as belonging to any known language,<sup about="#mwt51" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGager19929_16-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEGager19929"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEGager19929-16"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"Sfn ","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":" Gager "},"2":{"wt":" 1992 "},"p":{"wt":"9"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGager19929-16" id="mwfQ"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwfg"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwfw">[</span>16<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwgA">]</span></span></a></sup> and are commonly associated with curse tablets. Anthropologist <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanley_J._Tambiah" title="Stanley J. Tambiah" class="mw-redirect" id="mwgQ">Stanley J. Tambiah</a> proposed in 1968 that such words were intended to represent "the language that demons can understand".<sup about="#mwt54" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGager19929_16-1" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEGager19929"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEGager19929-16"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"Sfn ","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":" Gager "},"2":{"wt":" 1992 "},"p":{"wt":"9"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGager19929-16" id="mwgg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwgw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwhA">[</span>16<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwhQ">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

<p id="mwhg">Scholars from antiquity, like Christian philosopher <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clement_of_Alexandria" title="Clement of Alexandria" id="mwhw">Clement of Alexandria</a> (ca. 200 CE), believed that human language was not appropriate for addressing the gods. Therefore, some of the inscriptions of these curse tablets are not easily translatable, because they were "invocations and secret names" which would only be understood by the spirits themselves.  Another possibility is that curse tablets were produced by professionals who wished to lend their art a degree of mystique through the use of an apparently secret language that only they could understand. In support of this theory, at least some tablets appear to have blank spaces instead of a name for the target, suggesting they were prepared in advance, and that the desired target's name would be added on behalf of the customer.<sup about="#mwt57" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGager199210_17-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Transclusion mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"group":"","name":"FOOTNOTEGager199210"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-FOOTNOTEGager199210-17"},"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"Sfn ","href":"./Template:Sfn"},"params":{"1":{"wt":" Gager "},"2":{"wt":" 1992 "},"p":{"wt":"10"}},"i":0}}]}'><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGager199210-17" id="mwiA"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwiQ"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwig">[</span>17<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwiw">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

<p id="mwjA">The element of mystique was given through a number of ways along with <i id="mwjQ">voces mysticae.</i> Professionals and laypeople alike would utilize <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palindrome" title="Palindrome" id="mwjg">palindromes</a> as well as <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boustrophedon" title="Boustrophedon" id="mwjw">boustrophedon</a>. Pictures and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charaktêres" title="Charaktêres" id="mwkA">charakteres</a> gave further allure to the tablets, and occasionally specific formulae would be used and reused to relay a specifically intended tone. There were also frequent invocations of Egyptian gods and goddesses, archangels, and other biblical figures as a result of the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syncretism" title="Syncretism" id="mwkQ">syncretism</a> that occurred over time throughout the Mediterranean.</p>

</section><section data-mw-section-id="3" id="mwkg" aria-labelledby="Historiography"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Historiography">Historiography</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Curse_tablet&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Historiography"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p id="mwkw">The Greco-Roman society believed in using magic to control the natural world.<sup about="#mwt60" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-18" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-18"}}'><a href="#cite_note-18" id="mwlA"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwlQ"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwlg">[</span>18<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwlw">]</span></span></a></sup> This practice was common among all members of society, irrespective of their economic or social status. Approximately 1,600 curse tablets have been discovered, most of which are inscribed in Greek. Notably, 220 of these tablets were found in <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attica" title="Attica" id="mwmA">Attica</a>.<sup about="#mwt61" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-see_Ankarloo,_Bengt._p.3_19-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"see Ankarloo, Bengt. p.3"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-see_Ankarloo,_Bengt._p.3-19"}}'><a href="#cite_note-see_Ankarloo,_Bengt._p.3-19" id="mwmQ"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwmg"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwmw">[</span>19<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwnA">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

<p id="mwnQ">The first set of curse tablets to be discovered came from the city of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selinunte" title="Selinunte" id="mwng">Selinus</a> in <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sicily" title="Sicily" id="mwnw">Sicily</a>.  A total of twenty-two tablets were found, mostly coming from the early fifth century, and directed toward someone that the user was suing.<sup about="#mwt63" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-20" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-20"}}'><a href="#cite_note-20" id="mwoA"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwoQ"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwog">[</span>20<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwow">]</span></span></a></sup> While the ancient Greeks may have feared the power of these tablets, some historians<link rel="mw:PageProp/Category" href="./Category:All_articles_with_specifically_marked_weasel-worded_phrases" about="#mwt62" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mwpA" data-mw='{"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"who","href":"./Template:Who"},"params":{"date":{"wt":"March 2011"}},"i":0}}]}'/><link rel="mw:PageProp/Category" href="./Category:Articles_with_specifically_marked_weasel-worded_phrases_from_March_2011" about="#mwt62"/><sup class="noprint Inline-Template " style="white-space:nowrap;" about="#mwt62" id="mwpQ"><span typeof="mw:Entity">[</span><i><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Manual_of_Style/Words_to_watch#Unsupported_attributions" title="Wikipedia:Manual of Style/Words to watch"><span title="The material near this tag possibly uses too-vague attribution or weasel words. (March 2011)">who?</span></a></i><span typeof="mw:Entity">]</span></sup> have compared the tablets to modern <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Profanity" title="Profanity" id="mwpg">swearing</a>, arguing that they were produced in a fit of anger, in envy towards a business competitor or athletic opponent, or in an unhealthy obsession toward a person of romantic interest.</p>

<p id="mwpw">When research first began on the topic of curse tablets, there was serious doubt that these types of artifacts truly came from ancient Greek society. <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/E._R._Dodds" title="E. R. Dodds" id="mwqA">E. R. Dodds</a>, a professor of Greek at <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxford" title="Oxford" id="mwqQ">Oxford</a>, was one of the first scholars to begin studying the topic of magic or superstition in ancient Greece,<sup about="#mwt64" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-see_Green,_Peter._pg._44_21-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{"name":"see Green, Peter. pg. 44"},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-see_Green,_Peter._pg._44-21"}}'><a href="#cite_note-see_Green,_Peter._pg._44-21" id="mwqg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwqw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwrA">[</span>21<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwrQ">]</span></span></a></sup> and others such as <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Green_(historian)" title="Peter Green (historian)" id="mwrg">Peter Green</a> have also studied this aspect of ancient Greek society.</p>

</section><section data-mw-section-id="4" id="mwrw" aria-labelledby="Erotic_magic"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Erotic_magic">Erotic magic</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Curse_tablet&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Erotic magic"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p id="mwsA">The use of erotic curses became especially popular during the Hellenistic period of Mediterranean history. Scholars have debated the possible motivations for using <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Love_magic" title="Love magic" id="mwsQ">erotic magic</a>, including unrequited love, sexual control of the intended target, financial gain, and social advancement. The love spells used were similar in design around the Mediterranean world,<sup about="#mwt66" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-22" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-22"}}'><a href="#cite_note-22" id="mwsg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwsw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwtA">[</span>22<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwtQ">]</span></span></a></sup> and could be adjusted to different situations, users and intended victims. One notable type of curse was a "Diakopai", a separation spell intended to drive away rivals by making them repulsive. Another type of curse was an "Agogai", a spell that was intended to bind its target to oneself. Recent scholarship has shown that women used curse tablets for erotic magic much more than originally thought, although they were still in a minority.<link rel="mw:PageProp/Category" href="./Category:All_articles_with_unsourced_statements" about="#mwt65" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mwtg" data-mw='{"parts":[{"template":{"target":{"wt":"Citation needed","href":"./Template:Citation_needed"},"params":{"date":{"wt":"March 2011"}},"i":0}}]}'/><link rel="mw:PageProp/Category" href="./Category:Articles_with_unsourced_statements_from_March_2011" about="#mwt65"/><sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;" about="#mwt65" id="mwtw"><span typeof="mw:Entity">[</span><i><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (March 2011)">citation needed</span></a></i><span typeof="mw:Entity">]</span></sup></p>

<p id="mwuA">There is also debate over the type of women that men were trying to attract with these spells.  Some scholars subscribe to the idea of men trying to make fair, chaste women become filled with desire for them, while others argue that men were trying to control women whom they thought to be sexually active for their own personal benefit.<sup about="#mwt67" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-23" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-23"}}'><a href="#cite_note-23" id="mwuQ"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwug"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwuw">[</span>23<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwvA">]</span></span></a></sup> <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christopher_A._Faraone" title="Christopher A. Faraone" id="mwvQ">Christopher A. Faraone</a> considered the spells to fall into two distinct categories; spells used for inducing passion and spells used for encouraging affection.<sup about="#mwt68" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-24" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw='{"name":"ref","attrs":{},"body":{"id":"mw-reference-text-cite_note-24"}}'><a href="#cite_note-24" id="mwvg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwvw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwwA">[</span>24<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwwQ">]</span></span></a></sup> Men, according to Faraone, were the primary users of the passion-inducing spells, while women were the main users of the affection spells.</p>
